<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Exportin-5: Ran-GTP-Dependent Nuclear Export of Pre-miRNA Hairpins to Cytoplasm - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism of microRNA (miRNA) biogenesis, specifically the role of Exportin-5 (CRM1) and Ran-GTP in the nuclear export of pre-miRNA hairpin structures. It also assesses the ability to link a genetic defect in this pathway to a specific clinical phenotype. (考察miRNA生物发生机制，特别是Exportin-5 (CRM1)和Ran-GTP在pre-miRNA发夹结构核外转运中的作用。同时考察将该通路中的基因缺陷与特定临床表型联系起来的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric neurology clinic with developmental delay, hypotonia, seizures refractory to multiple anti-epileptic drugs, and characteristic facial features including a prominent forehead, hypertelorism, and a small chin. His parents report that he has been unable to walk independently and has limited expressive language. Family history reveals that his older sister died in infancy due to severe neurological complications. Karyotype analysis is normal (46,XY). Brain MRI shows diffuse white matter abnormalities and cerebellar hypoplasia. A targeted gene panel for developmental delay identified a homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Gly412Asp) in the *CRM1* gene. Biochemical analysis of patient fibroblasts reveals a significant reduction in mature miRNA levels in the cytoplasm compared to controls, while nuclear levels of pre-miRNA are elevated. *In vitro* studies using the patient's fibroblasts show impaired binding of pre-miRNA hairpin structures to Exportin-5 in the presence of Ran-GTP.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's phenotype?

(A) Impaired Dicer processing of pre-miRNA into mature miRNA due to defective RNA helicase activity.
(B) Defective nuclear import of Drosha and DGCR8 complex into the nucleus, preventing initial pre-miRNA processing.
(C) Reduced activity of Argonaute (AGO) proteins, leading to inefficient loading of mature miRNA onto the RISC complex.
(D) Impaired nuclear export of pre-miRNA hairpin structures due to defective Exportin-5 function, resulting in cytoplasmic accumulation of mature miRNA.
(E) Defective terminal uridylation of pre-miRNA by HUA2/Dyskerin complex, leading to premature degradation of pre-miRNA in the nucleus.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, refractory seizures, dysmorphic features, and early death of a sibling. This suggests a severe, likely genetic, neurodevelopmental disorder.
- **Lab Findings:** Normal karyotype rules out large chromosomal abnormalities. Brain MRI shows white matter abnormalities and cerebellar hypoplasia, consistent with neurological dysfunction.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous missense mutation (p.Gly412Asp) in the *CRM1* gene. *CRM1* encodes Exportin-5 (also known as CRM1).
- **Biochemical Findings:** Reduced cytoplasmic mature miRNA levels and elevated nuclear pre-miRNA levels in patient fibroblasts. This indicates a block in the pathway leading from nuclear pre-miRNA to cytoplasmic mature miRNA.
- **Functional Assay:** Impaired binding of pre-miRNA hairpin structures to Exportin-5 in the presence of Ran-GTP in patient fibroblasts. This directly implicates Exportin-5 function.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the phenotype based on the findings.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **miRNA Biogenesis:** miRNAs are small non-coding RNAs that regulate gene expression post-transcriptionally. Their biogenesis involves several steps:
    1.  **Transcription:** Primary miRNA transcripts (pri-miRNAs) are transcribed by RNA polymerase II in the nucleus.
    2.  **Processing in Nucleus:** The microprocessor complex (Drosha and DGCR8) cleaves pri-miRNAs into precursor miRNAs (pre-miRNAs), which are ~70 nucleotide hairpin structures.
    3.  **Nuclear Export:** Pre-miRNAs are actively transported out of the nucleus into the cytoplasm. This step is crucial and requires the Ran-GTP-dependent nuclear export receptor Exportin-5 (CRM1). Exportin-5 binds to the pre-miRNA hairpin, and this binding is facilitated by Ran-GTP. The Exportin-5/pre-miRNA/Ran-GTP complex is then transported through the nuclear pore complex (NPC) into the cytoplasm. In the cytoplasm, Ran-GAP hydrolyzes GTP to GDP, causing the dissociation of the complex and the release of pre-miRNA.
    4.  **Processing in Cytoplasm:** In the cytoplasm, the enzyme Dicer, along with its partner TRBP (in humans), cleaves the pre-miRNA hairpin into a short miRNA duplex (~22 nucleotides).
    5.  **RISC Loading:** One strand of the miRNA duplex (the guide strand) is loaded onto the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC), primarily by Argonaute (AGO) proteins. The other strand (the passenger strand) is usually degraded.
    6.  **Target Recognition & Silencing:** The miRNA within the RISC guides the complex to target mRNAs, typically in the 3' UTR, leading to translational repression or mRNA degradation.
- **Role of Exportin-5 (CRM1):** Exportin-5 is the only known cellular receptor for pre-miRNA and tRNA. Its function is essential for the nuclear export of these molecules. The export process is dependent on the GTP-bound form of Ran (Ran-GTP), which is highly concentrated in the nucleus.
- **Patient's Defect:** The patient has a homozygous missense mutation (p.Gly412Asp) in *CRM1*, leading to defective Exportin-5 function. The *in vitro* studies confirm impaired binding of pre-miRNA to the mutant Exportin-5.
- **Consequences:** Defective Exportin-5 function impairs the nuclear export of pre-miRNA hairpins. This leads to an accumulation of pre-miRNA in the nucleus and a corresponding deficiency of mature miRNA in the cytoplasm. The lack of mature miRNAs disrupts the regulation of numerous target genes, particularly those involved in neuronal development and function, explaining the patient's severe neurodevelopmental phenotype. The clinical presentation (developmental delay, hypotonia, seizures, dysmorphic features, white matter abnormalities) is consistent with a severe miRNA processing defect.
- **Linking to Answer Choice (D):** The findings directly support mechanism (D): impaired nuclear export of pre-miRNA due to defective Exportin-5 function, resulting in cytoplasmic accumulation of mature miRNA (which is actually *reduced* in the cytoplasm, but the *cause* is the export defect). The question asks for the mechanism explaining the phenotype, which is the impaired export.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired Dicer processing: Dicer is responsible for cleaving pre-miRNA into mature miRNA in the *cytoplasm*. While defects in Dicer can cause similar phenotypes, the patient's findings (elevated nuclear pre-miRNA, reduced cytoplasmic mature miRNA, defective Exportin-5 binding) point to a block *before* cytoplasmic processing. Dicer requires ATP, not Ran-GTP. This option describes a cytoplasmic defect, not the nuclear export defect identified.
- (B) Defective nuclear import of Drosha/DGCR8: Drosha and DGCR8 form the microprocessor complex, which processes pri-miRNA into pre-miRNA *within the nucleus*. Defects in their nuclear import would prevent the initial processing step, leading to accumulation of pri-miRNA, not pre-miRNA. The patient has elevated pre-miRNA, indicating that initial processing by Drosha/DGCR8 is likely occurring. This option describes a defect in the *initial* nuclear processing step.
- (C) Reduced activity of Argonaute (AGO) proteins: AGO proteins are components of the RISC complex and are responsible for loading mature miRNA and targeting mRNAs in the *cytoplasm*. Defects in AGO function would impair the silencing activity of mature miRNAs but would not explain the accumulation of pre-miRNA in the nucleus or the reduced levels of mature miRNA. This option describes a defect in the *final* silencing step.
- (E) Defective terminal uridylation by HUA2/Dyskerin: HUA2 (also known as Dyskerin) adds a terminal monouridylate residue to the 3' end of pre-miRNA in the nucleus. This modification is important for efficient Dicer processing. Defects in HUA2/Dyskerin cause dyskeratosis congenita and can lead to impaired miRNA processing, but the primary defect is in the modification of pre-miRNA, not its export. The patient's defect is specifically in the export mechanism involving Exportin-5.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the sequential steps of miRNA biogenesis, including the critical role of Exportin-5 and Ran-GTP in the nuclear export of pre-miRNA hairpins. Recognize that defects at any step can lead to disease, but specific genetic mutations and biochemical findings pinpoint the exact location of the block.
- **Differentials:** Defects in other steps of miRNA biogenesis (Drosha/DGCR8 processing, Dicer processing, AGO function) can cause similar neurodevelopmental phenotypes. However, the specific mutation in *CRM1* (Exportin-5), coupled with the biochemical findings (nuclear pre-miRNA accumulation, cytoplasmic mature miRNA deficiency, impaired Exportin-5 binding), uniquely points to a defect in nuclear export.

---

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Dicer Endonuclease: Cytoplasmic Cleavage of Pre-miRNA into ~22-Nucleotide Mature miRNA Duplex - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the biogenesis pathway of microRNAs (miRNAs), specifically the role of the Dicer endonuclease in cleaving pre-miRNA hairpin structures into mature, functional miRNA duplexes in the cytoplasm. It also assesses the ability to connect a specific genetic defect (loss-of-function mutation in DICER1) to a clinical phenotype (sensorineural hearing loss, developmental delay, and potentially other features).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric clinic with delayed speech development and significant sensorineural hearing loss, noted since infancy. His parents report that he has been meeting most motor milestones but exhibits some cognitive delays, particularly in language comprehension. Family history is unremarkable for similar conditions or genetic disorders. Physical examination reveals normal facial features, normal growth parameters, and no obvious dysmorphic features. Audiological testing confirms bilateral, severe, high-frequency sensorineural hearing loss. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous missense mutation in the *DICER1* gene (c.1234G>A, p.Gly412Asp) located on chromosome 17q21.31. This mutation is predicted to disrupt the RNase III domain of the Dicer1 protein. Further investigation shows significantly reduced levels of mature miRNAs in the patient's peripheral blood lymphocytes compared to age-matched controls, particularly miRNAs involved in inner ear development and neuronal function.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Impaired export of pre-mRNA from the nucleus due to defective splicing factors, leading to reduced translation of proteins essential for cochlear function.
(B) Defective processing of primary microRNAs (pri-miRNAs) by the Drosha complex in the nucleus, resulting in accumulation of unprocessed pri-miRNAs and reduced levels of mature miRNAs.
(C) Impaired cytoplasmic cleavage of pre-miRNA hairpin structures by the Dicer endonuclease, leading to decreased levels of mature miRNA duplexes and subsequent dysregulation of target gene expression.
(D) Aberrant incorporation of mutated Dicer1 protein into the RISC complex, leading to non-specific cleavage of target mRNAs and inappropriate gene silencing.
(E) Defective binding of mature miRNAs to their target mRNAs due to altered seed region sequences, resulting in loss of translational repression of key developmental genes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with delayed speech, sensorineural hearing loss (since infancy), cognitive/language delays. This points towards a developmental disorder affecting the central nervous system and/or sensory organs (inner ear).
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Gly412Asp) in the *DICER1* gene. *DICER1* encodes the Dicer1 enzyme. The mutation affects the RNase III domain, which is crucial for Dicer's enzymatic activity.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Reduced levels of mature miRNAs in peripheral blood lymphocytes. This directly implicates a defect in miRNA biogenesis.
- **Connecting Clues:** The *DICER1* gene encodes the Dicer1 enzyme, a key component of the miRNA biogenesis pathway. Dicer1 is primarily cytoplasmic and responsible for cleaving pre-miRNA hairpin structures into mature miRNA duplexes. The mutation affects the RNase III domain, suggesting a loss of function. Reduced mature miRNAs confirm a defect in this pathway. The clinical phenotype (hearing loss, developmental delay) is consistent with known consequences of Dicer1 deficiency, as miRNAs regulate genes involved in inner ear development, neuronal differentiation, and synaptic plasticity.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **miRNA Biogenesis:** miRNAs are small non-coding RNAs that regulate gene expression post-transcriptionally. The process begins in the nucleus with transcription of pri-miRNA by RNA polymerase II. Pri-miRNA is processed by the microprocessor complex (containing Drosha and DGCR8) into a ~70-nucleotide pre-miRNA hairpin structure.
- **Nuclear Export:** Pre-miRNA is exported from the nucleus to the cytoplasm by Exportin-5.
- **Cytoplasmic Processing:** In the cytoplasm, the pre-miRNA hairpin is recognized and cleaved by the Dicer enzyme (Dicer1 in humans) along with its associated protein, TRBP (TAR RNA-binding protein). Dicer1 is an RNase III endonuclease.
- **Mature miRNA Duplex:** Dicer1 cleaves the pre-miRNA hairpin, generating a ~22-nucleotide miRNA duplex with characteristic 2-nucleotide 3' overhangs.
- **RISC Loading:** One strand of the duplex (the guide strand) is loaded into the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC), while the other strand (the passenger strand, miRNA*) is typically degraded.
- **Target Recognition & Silencing:** The miRNA within the RISC guides the complex to target mRNAs based on sequence complementarity, primarily through interactions between the miRNA seed region (nucleotides 2-8) and the 3' untranslated region (3'UTR) of the target mRNA. This leads to translational repression or mRNA degradation, depending on the degree of complementarity.
- **Dicer1 Function:** Dicer1 is essential for the final step of miRNA maturation in the cytoplasm. A loss-of-function mutation in *DICER1*, as described in the vignette (p.Gly412Asp affecting the RNase III domain), would impair its ability to cleave pre-miRNA. This leads to reduced levels of mature miRNA duplexes and subsequent dysregulation of the expression of numerous target genes involved in development, cell differentiation, and tissue homeostasis, explaining the patient's phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired export of pre-mRNA from the nucleus due to defective splicing factors. This mechanism relates to pre-mRNA processing and transport, not miRNA biogenesis. While splicing defects can cause disease, this is not the mechanism affected by a *DICER1* mutation. Dicer acts on pre-miRNA, not pre-mRNA.
- (B): Defective processing of primary microRNAs (pri-miRNAs) by the Drosha complex in the nucleus. This step occurs *before* pre-miRNA formation and involves the Drosha enzyme in the nucleus. The *DICER1* mutation affects cytoplasmic processing by Dicer, not nuclear processing by Drosha. While defects in Drosha can also cause miRNA processing issues, the vignette specifies a *DICER1* mutation and reduced *mature* miRNAs, pointing to a later step.
- (D): Aberrant incorporation of mutated Dicer1 protein into the RISC complex, leading to non-specific cleavage of target mRNAs. Dicer1's primary role is to process pre-miRNA into mature miRNA duplexes. It is not a component of the RISC complex, which contains Argonaute proteins. Furthermore, the mutation is predicted to impair its RNase activity, not cause non-specific cleavage. The problem is the *lack* of mature miRNA production, not aberrant RISC function.
- (E): Defective binding of mature miRNAs to their target mRNAs due to altered seed region sequences. This mechanism relates to the interaction between the mature miRNA and its target mRNA. While mutations in the miRNA seed region can affect target binding, the primary defect in this case is the *production* of mature miRNAs due to the impaired function of Dicer1. The vignette states reduced levels of mature miRNAs, indicating a problem upstream of target binding.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the distinct steps in miRNA biogenesis (nuclear processing by Drosha, cytoplasmic processing by Dicer, RISC loading, target silencing) and the specific roles of key enzymes like Drosha and Dicer. Recognize that mutations in genes encoding these enzymes can lead to developmental disorders.
- **Differentials:** Consider other genetic causes of sensorineural hearing loss and developmental delay. However, the specific *DICER1* mutation and the finding of reduced mature miRNAs strongly point to a defect in miRNA biogenesis as the underlying mechanism. Other miRNA processing defects (e.g., *DGCR8*, *EXPORTIN5*, *DICER*) can cause similar phenotypes.

---

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: RNA-Induced Silencing Complex (RISC): Argonaute (AGO2) Loading and miRNA Strand Selection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The mechanism of miRNA biogenesis and function, specifically the loading of the miRNA duplex onto the RISC complex, the role of Argonaute 2 (AGO2) in strand selection and target mRNA cleavage, and the consequences of disrupting this process. (miRNA 生物发生机制及功能，特别是miRNA双链体加载到RISC复合物上，Argonaute 2 (AGO2) 在链选择和靶mRNA切割中的作用，以及破坏该过程的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the clinic with progressive muscle weakness and fatigue over the past 6 months. Her symptoms began insidiously and have worsened, particularly affecting proximal muscles. She denies any history of trauma, fever, rash, or recent infections. Family history is unremarkable for neuromuscular disorders. Physical examination reveals bilateral ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, and proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 3/5 in upper limbs, 4/5 in lower limbs). Reflexes are normal. Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are markedly elevated at 15,000 U/L (normal < 200 U/L). Electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy reveals ragged red fibers and increased mitochondrial density. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Gly412Asp) in the *AGO2* gene. Subsequent biochemical analysis of patient fibroblasts shows a significant reduction (approximately 80%) in the ability of endogenous microRNA-1 (miR-1) to cleave its target mRNA, *SOD1*, compared to control fibroblasts. The *SOD1* mRNA levels are significantly elevated in the patient's fibroblasts.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the patient's clinical presentation and biochemical findings?

(A) The *AGO2* mutation impairs the binding of Dicer to the miRNA duplex, preventing the generation of the miRNA:miRNA* duplex required for RISC loading.
(B) The *AGO2* mutation disrupts the interaction between the miRNA duplex and the Argonaute protein, preventing the loading of the miRNA duplex onto the RISC complex.
(C) The *AGO2* mutation alters the strand selection process within the RISC complex, leading to preferential loading of the passenger strand (miRNA*) instead of the guide strand (miRNA), resulting in loss of target mRNA regulation.
(D) The *AGO2* mutation impairs the catalytic activity of the Argonaute protein (specifically its "slicer" activity), preventing the cleavage of target mRNAs even after correct miRNA loading and strand selection.
(E) The *AGO2* mutation leads to the degradation of mature miRNAs by increasing their accessibility to exonucleases, thereby reducing the overall pool of functional miRNAs available for RISC loading.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with progressive proximal muscle weakness, fatigue, ptosis, ophthalmoplegia. This constellation of symptoms points towards a myopathy, potentially mitochondrial or neuromuscular junction related, given the ptosis/ophthalmoplegia.
- **Lab Findings:** Markedly elevated CK (15,000 U/L) confirms muscle damage. EMG shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy shows ragged red fibers and increased mitochondrial density, strongly suggesting a mitochondrial myopathy.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous missense mutation (c.1234G>A, p.Gly412Asp) in the *AGO2* gene. *AGO2* encodes Argonaute 2, a key component of the RNA-Induced Silencing Complex (RISC). This immediately links the myopathy to a defect in miRNA processing or function.
- **Biochemical Findings:** Reduced ability of miR-1 to cleave its target mRNA, *SOD1*, in patient fibroblasts. Elevated *SOD1* mRNA levels in patient fibroblasts. miR-1 is a muscle-specific miRNA crucial for mitochondrial function and muscle development. *SOD1* encodes superoxide dismutase 1, a mitochondrial antioxidant enzyme. Reduced miR-1 activity leads to *SOD1* overexpression, which can be toxic, particularly to mitochondria, explaining the mitochondrial myopathy phenotype (ragged red fibers). The question asks for the molecular mechanism linking the *AGO2* mutation to the observed biochemical defect (impaired *SOD1* mRNA cleavage).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **miRNA Biogenesis:** miRNAs are transcribed as primary miRNAs (pri-miRNAs), processed by Drosha and DGCR8 into precursor miRNAs (pre-miRNAs), exported to the cytoplasm, and further processed by Dicer into a short miRNA duplex (approximately 22 nucleotides).
- **RISC Assembly:** The miRNA duplex is unwound, and one strand (the guide strand, miRNA) is loaded onto the RISC complex. The other strand (the passenger strand, miRNA*) is typically degraded. The RISC complex contains several proteins, but the core catalytic component is an Argonaute protein (AGO). In mammals, AGO2 is the primary catalytic Argonaute protein.
- **Strand Selection:** The guide strand (miRNA) directs the RISC complex to target mRNAs based on sequence complementarity. The passenger strand (miRNA*) is usually discarded, although it can sometimes function as a miRNA. The selection of the guide strand over the passenger strand is crucial and occurs within the RISC complex, involving interactions with the Argonaute protein (AGO2).
- **Target mRNA Regulation:** Once the RISC complex binds to the target mRNA, AGO2 uses its "slicer" activity (an RNase H-like domain) to cleave the target mRNA if there is near-perfect complementarity (common in animals). If complementarity is imperfect (common in animals), AGO2 can recruit other proteins to repress translation or promote mRNA degradation.
- **Patient's Mutation:** The patient has a missense mutation (Gly412Asp) in *AGO2*. This mutation is located within the catalytic domain of the protein.
- **Connecting the Dots:** The vignette states that the patient's fibroblasts show a reduced ability of miR-1 to cleave its target *SOD1* mRNA. This indicates a defect *after* the miRNA has been loaded onto the RISC complex and has found its target mRNA. The mutation in AGO2 is specifically linked to impaired *cleavage* of the target mRNA. The slicer activity of AGO2 is responsible for this cleavage. Therefore, the mutation likely impairs the catalytic activity of AGO2.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The *AGO2* mutation impairs the binding of Dicer to the miRNA duplex, preventing the generation of the miRNA:miRNA* duplex required for RISC loading. **Incorrect.** Dicer processes the pre-miRNA into the miRNA duplex. While AGO2 is involved in RISC assembly *after* Dicer processing, the primary defect described (impaired cleavage) points to a problem with AGO2's function within the assembled RISC, not its interaction with Dicer or the initial duplex generation. The vignette describes a defect in the *cleavage* step, which occurs *after* Dicer processing and RISC loading.
- (B): The *AGO2* mutation disrupts the interaction between the miRNA duplex and the Argonaute protein, preventing the loading of the miRNA duplex onto the RISC complex. **Incorrect.** If the miRNA duplex could not load onto the RISC complex due to a defect in interaction with AGO2, then *both* the guide strand and passenger strand would be affected, and the target mRNA cleavage would not occur. However, the vignette states that miR-1 *can* bind its target *SOD1* mRNA, but the *cleavage* is reduced. This implies the miRNA is loaded onto the RISC, but the subsequent step (cleavage) is impaired.
- (C): The *AGO2* mutation alters the strand selection process within the RISC complex, leading to preferential loading of the passenger strand (miRNA*) instead of the guide strand (miRNA), resulting in loss of target mRNA regulation. **Incorrect.** While AGO2 is crucial for strand selection, the primary defect described in the vignette is the *failure to cleave* the target mRNA *after* the guide strand (miR-1) has been loaded and has bound the target. Altered strand selection would mean the wrong strand is loaded, leading to a *loss* of regulation, but the vignette describes a *reduced* ability to cleave the target mRNA by the *correct* miRNA (miR-1). The mutation is described as Gly412Asp, which is within the catalytic domain, strongly suggesting a defect in the slicer activity rather than strand selection.
- (E): The *AGO2* mutation leads to the degradation of mature miRNAs by increasing their accessibility to exonucleases, thereby reducing the overall pool of functional miRNAs available for RISC loading. **Incorrect.** This mechanism would lead to a general reduction in miRNA levels, affecting multiple targets. The vignette specifically points to a defect in the cleavage of *one* specific target mRNA (*SOD1*) by *one* specific miRNA (miR-1), suggesting a problem with the function of the loaded RISC complex rather than a global reduction in miRNA availability.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the role of AGO2 within the RISC complex, specifically its catalytic "slicer" activity responsible for cleaving target mRNAs. Mutations in AGO2 can lead to various phenotypes depending on the specific mutation and the affected miRNAs. This case highlights how defects in miRNA function can cause mitochondrial myopathies. Differential diagnoses for mitochondrial myopathies include defects in oxidative phosphorylation components, tRNA synthesis, or other nuclear-encoded mitochondrial proteins. This case specifically links a mitochondrial myopathy to a defect in miRNA-mediated regulation of *SOD1*.

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: miRNA Mechanism of Action - Translational Repression & mRNA Decay
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: MicroRNAs (miRNAs) regulate gene expression post-transcriptionally by binding to complementary sequences, typically in the 3' untranslated region (3' UTR) of target mRNAs, leading to translational repression and/or mRNA degradation. (miRNA 通过与靶 mRNA 的 3' 非翻译区 (3' UTR) 互补配对，从而调节基因表达，导致翻译抑制和/或 mRNA 分解。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive shortness of breath and fatigue over the past 6 months. He has a 40-pack-year smoking history and was diagnosed with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) 5 years ago. Physical examination reveals decreased breath sounds bilaterally and mild peripheral edema. An echocardiogram shows right ventricular hypertrophy and pulmonary hypertension. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *DICER1* gene. Further investigation reveals significantly reduced levels of mature miRNAs in his lung tissue biopsies. A researcher is studying the effect of this *DICER1* mutation on miRNA function in lung epithelial cells. They find that a specific miRNA, miR-146a, normally targets the mRNA of interleukin-6 (IL-6), a pro-inflammatory cytokine. In cells with the *DICER1* mutation, miR-146a levels are low, and IL-6 mRNA levels are elevated. The researcher then introduces a synthetic, chemically modified version of miR-146a that is resistant to degradation but retains its ability to bind to the 3' UTR of IL-6 mRNA. They observe that this synthetic miR-146a binds imperfectly to the 3' UTR of IL-6 mRNA, leading to decreased protein translation of IL-6 and subsequent degradation of the IL-6 mRNA. What is the primary mechanism by which this synthetic miR-146a exerts its effect on IL-6 mRNA?

(A) Activation of RNA polymerase II to increase IL-6 mRNA transcription.
(B) Recruitment of DNA methyltransferases to methylate the IL-6 promoter region, inhibiting transcription.
(C) Inhibition of ribosome binding and translocation, leading to decreased IL-6 protein synthesis, and subsequent recruitment of deadenylation complexes leading to mRNA decay.
(D) Direct cleavage of the IL-6 mRNA molecule at the miRNA binding site by the RISC complex.
(E) Inhibition of mRNA export from the nucleus, preventing IL-6 protein translation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male smoker with COPD, shortness of breath, fatigue, right ventricular hypertrophy, pulmonary hypertension. This suggests chronic inflammation and lung damage.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous *DICER1* mutation. DICER1 is an endoribonuclease crucial for processing pre-miRNAs into mature miRNAs. Mutations often lead to reduced miRNA levels.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Reduced mature miRNA levels in lung tissue. This confirms the functional consequence of the *DICER1* mutation.
- **Specific miRNA/Target:** miR-146a normally targets IL-6 mRNA. IL-6 is a pro-inflammatory cytokine.
- **Effect of *DICER1* mutation:** Low miR-146a levels lead to elevated IL-6 mRNA levels, consistent with loss of miRNA-mediated repression.
- **Experimental Intervention:** Introduction of synthetic miR-146a (degradation-resistant).
- **Observed Effect:** Synthetic miR-146a binds imperfectly to IL-6 mRNA 3' UTR, decreases IL-6 protein translation, and causes IL-6 mRNA degradation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *primary mechanism* by which the synthetic miR-146a exerts its effect.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **miRNA Biogenesis:** miRNAs are transcribed as primary transcripts (pri-miRNAs), processed by Drosha/DGCR8 into precursor miRNAs (pre-miRNAs), exported to the cytoplasm, and further processed by Dicer (specifically Dicer-1 in the cytoplasm) into mature miRNA duplexes. One strand is loaded into the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC).
- **Mechanism of Action:** Mature miRNAs guide the RISC complex to target mRNAs. The binding site is typically in the 3' UTR of the target mRNA.
- **Binding Specificity:** miRNA binding to the target mRNA is often *imperfect* (not perfectly complementary), especially in animals. Perfect complementarity usually leads to mRNA cleavage (common in plants and some invertebrates). Imperfect complementarity in animals leads primarily to translational repression and/or mRNA destabilization.
- **Translational Repression:** Imperfect binding can inhibit ribosome binding, prevent ribosome translocation along the mRNA, or cause premature termination of translation.
- **mRNA Decay:** Imperfect binding can recruit deadenylation complexes (e.g., CCR4-NOT complex) that remove the poly(A) tail, leading to mRNA destabilization and degradation by exonucleases. It can also recruit decapping enzymes.
- **In this case:** The synthetic miR-146a binds *imperfectly* to the IL-6 mRNA 3' UTR. This imperfect binding leads to both decreased protein translation (translational repression) and subsequent mRNA degradation. The question asks for the *primary* mechanism, and both translational repression and mRNA decay are key consequences of imperfect binding. Option (C) encompasses both aspects: inhibition of translation (ribosome binding/translocation) and subsequent mRNA decay (recruitment of deadenylation complexes).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** miRNAs act post-transcriptionally. They do not affect RNA polymerase II activity or transcription initiation. This option describes a mechanism related to transcriptional regulation, not miRNA function.
- (B): **Incorrect.** miRNAs do not directly interact with DNA or DNA methyltransferases. DNA methylation typically occurs at CpG islands in promoter regions and leads to transcriptional silencing. This is a mechanism of epigenetic transcriptional regulation, not miRNA action.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Direct cleavage of the target mRNA by the RISC complex occurs primarily when there is *perfect* or near-perfect complementarity between the miRNA and the mRNA target site. This is more common in plants and some invertebrates. In mammals (like humans), miRNA binding is typically imperfect, leading to translational repression and mRNA decay, not cleavage. The vignette explicitly states the binding is *imperfect*.
- (E): **Incorrect.** miRNAs do not directly inhibit mRNA export from the nucleus. mRNA export is a complex process involving nuclear pore complexes and specific transport factors. miRNA action occurs in the cytoplasm after the mRNA has been exported.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** miRNAs regulate gene expression post-transcriptionally via imperfect base-pairing with the 3' UTR of target mRNAs, leading primarily to translational repression and mRNA decay in mammals. Perfect pairing leads to mRNA cleavage (less common in mammals).
- **Differentials:** Distinguish miRNA mechanism from siRNA mechanism (siRNAs typically have perfect complementarity and induce mRNA cleavage), transcriptional regulation mechanisms (affecting DNA/RNA polymerase), and epigenetic mechanisms (like DNA methylation or histone modification). Remember that DICER is involved in processing both miRNAs and siRNAs.

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Oncogenic miRNAs (OncomiRs): miR-21 and miR-155 Overexpression Downregulating Tumor Suppressors - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of microRNAs (miRNAs) as oncogenes (OncomiRs) and their mechanism of action in downregulating tumor suppressor genes, leading to uncontrolled cell proliferation and tumor development. Specifically, the roles of miR-21 and miR-155 in various cancers.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician with a 3-month history of progressively worsening fatigue, unintentional weight loss (15 lbs), and night sweats. He has a significant smoking history (40 pack-years) and was diagnosed with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) 5 years ago. Physical examination reveals bilateral supraclavicular lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. A chest X-ray shows a large mass in the right upper lobe with mediastinal involvement. Biopsy of the lung mass confirms diagnosis of small cell lung cancer (SCLC). Subsequent molecular profiling of the tumor tissue reveals significantly elevated levels of miR-21 and miR-155 compared to adjacent non-tumor lung tissue. Further analysis shows that miR-21 directly targets the 3'UTR of *PTEN* mRNA, leading to decreased PTEN protein levels. Similarly, miR-155 targets the 3'UTR of *TP53* mRNA, resulting in reduced TP53 protein expression. Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains how the overexpression of miR-21 contributes to the malignant phenotype observed in this patient's SCLC?

(A) miR-21 promotes the translation of *MYC* mRNA, leading to increased MYC protein levels and cell cycle progression.
(B) miR-21 inhibits the degradation of *BCL2* mRNA, leading to increased BCL2 protein levels and resistance to apoptosis.
(C) miR-21 binds to the promoter region of the *PTEN* gene, inhibiting its transcription and reducing PTEN protein levels.
(D) miR-21 binds to the 3'UTR of *PTEN* mRNA, leading to its degradation or translational repression, thereby reducing PTEN protein levels.
(E) miR-21 acts as a decoy for *PTEN* protein, preventing it from interacting with its downstream targets and inhibiting its function.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male smoker with COPD presents with symptoms suggestive of malignancy (fatigue, weight loss, night sweats, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, lung mass).
- **Diagnosis:** Small cell lung cancer (SCLC), a known malignancy often associated with smoking.
- **Molecular Findings:** Elevated levels of miR-21 and miR-155 in tumor tissue compared to normal tissue.
- **Specific Mechanism:** miR-21 targets *PTEN* mRNA (3'UTR), leading to decreased PTEN protein. miR-155 targets *TP53* mRNA (3'UTR), leading to decreased TP53 protein.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which miR-21 overexpression contributes to the malignant phenotype, specifically focusing on its interaction with *PTEN*.
- **Key Clue:** The vignette explicitly states that miR-21 targets the 3'UTR of *PTEN* mRNA.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **MicroRNAs (miRNAs):** Small non-coding RNA molecules (~22 nucleotides) that regulate gene expression post-transcriptionally.
- **Mechanism of Action:** miRNAs bind to complementary sequences, primarily within the 3' untranslated region (3'UTR) of target messenger RNAs (mRNAs).
- **Consequences of Binding:** This binding typically leads to either mRNA degradation or translational repression (inhibition of protein synthesis).
- **OncomiRs:** Certain miRNAs are overexpressed in cancer and promote tumorigenesis by targeting tumor suppressor genes. miR-21 and miR-155 are well-established OncomiRs.
- **miR-21:** Overexpressed in various cancers, including SCLC. It targets multiple tumor suppressor genes, including *PTEN*, *TP53*, *PDCD4*, and *PDCD15*.
- **PTEN:** A tumor suppressor gene encoding a phosphatase that negatively regulates the PI3K/AKT signaling pathway. Loss of PTEN function leads to increased AKT activity, promoting cell survival, proliferation, and inhibiting apoptosis.
- **Targeting Mechanism:** miR-21 binds to the 3'UTR of *PTEN* mRNA. This interaction recruits RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC), leading to either degradation of the *PTEN* mRNA or inhibition of its translation into PTEN protein. The net effect is reduced PTEN protein levels.
- **Consequence in SCLC:** Reduced PTEN levels in SCLC cells lead to constitutive activation of the PI3K/AKT pathway, contributing to uncontrolled cell growth, survival, and resistance to therapy.
- **miR-155:** Another OncomiR overexpressed in SCLC. It targets *TP53* mRNA (also via the 3'UTR), leading to reduced TP53 protein levels. TP53 is a critical tumor suppressor involved in cell cycle arrest, DNA repair, and apoptosis. Its downregulation further promotes tumorigenesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** miR-21 primarily acts by *downregulating* tumor suppressors, not *upregulating* oncogenes like *MYC*. While miRNAs can sometimes target oncogenes, the established mechanism for miR-21's oncogenic role involves targeting tumor suppressors like PTEN and TP53. *MYC* is often upregulated through other mechanisms in cancer, such as gene amplification or transcription factor activation.
- (B): **Incorrect.** miR-21 targets *PTEN* mRNA, leading to *decreased* PTEN protein. PTEN is a phosphatase that *inhibits* the PI3K/AKT pathway. Decreased PTEN leads to *increased* AKT activity, which promotes cell survival by *inhibiting* apoptosis (partially through BCL2 regulation, but BCL2 itself is not the direct target of miR-21 in this context). miR-21 does not directly inhibit the degradation of *BCL2* mRNA; other miRNAs (like miR-217) are involved in BCL2 regulation.
- (C): **Incorrect.** miRNAs exert their regulatory effects primarily at the *post-transcriptional* level by binding to mRNA. They typically bind to the 3'UTR of target mRNAs, not the promoter region of the gene. Promoter binding would affect transcription, which is not the mechanism of action for miRNAs.
- (E): **Incorrect.** miRNAs function by binding to mRNA sequences, leading to mRNA degradation or translational repression. They do not act as decoys for proteins. Protein-protein interaction decoys are a different regulatory mechanism, not involving miRNAs.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand that miRNAs are key post-transcriptional regulators of gene expression. Certain miRNAs (OncomiRs) are overexpressed in cancer and promote tumorigenesis by targeting tumor suppressor genes, often via binding to the 3'UTR of their target mRNAs, leading to mRNA degradation or translational repression. miR-21 and miR-155 are classic examples.
- **Differentials:** Consider other mechanisms of gene regulation (transcriptional control, protein modification, protein degradation). Differentiate miRNA action from siRNA action (siRNAs typically cause mRNA cleavage). Recognize the roles of key tumor suppressors like PTEN and TP53 and how their downregulation contributes to cancer. Understand the PI3K/AKT pathway and its role in cell survival and proliferation.

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Tumor Suppressor miRNAs: miR-34 Family Downregulation Due to p53 Inactivation in Epithelial Cancers - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of p53 as a transcription factor regulating tumor suppressor microRNAs (specifically miR-34 family) and the consequences of p53 inactivation in cancer development, particularly epithelial cancers. (理解 p53 作为转录因子调控肿瘤抑制剂 microRNA (特别是 miR-34 家族) 的作用，以及 p53 失活在癌症发展中的后果，特别是上皮癌。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male smoker presents to his primary care physician with a persistent cough and shortness of breath for the past 3 months. He has a 40 pack-year smoking history. Physical examination reveals decreased breath sounds in the right lower lobe. A chest X-ray shows a large mass in the right lung, suspicious for malignancy. A CT-guided biopsy of the lung mass is performed. Histopathological examination confirms squamous cell carcinoma of the lung. Molecular analysis of the tumor tissue reveals a missense mutation in the *TP53* gene (R248Q), leading to a non-functional p53 protein. Further analysis of the tumor cells shows significantly reduced expression levels of miR-34a and miR-34b compared to adjacent non-cancerous lung tissue. Western blot analysis confirms decreased levels of the miR-34a protein (a component of the RISC complex) in the tumor cells. Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the reduced expression of miR-34a/b in this patient's tumor?

(A) The *TP53* mutation leads to increased methylation of the *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* gene promoters, causing transcriptional silencing.
(B) The non-functional p53 protein is unable to bind to the enhancer regions of the *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* genes, preventing their transcription.
(C) The *TP53* mutation results in the production of a dominant-negative p53 protein that sequesters transcription factors required for *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* transcription.
(D) The missense mutation in *TP53* causes misfolding and degradation of the p53 protein, leading to increased activity of histone deacetylases (HDACs) that repress *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* transcription.
(E) The non-functional p53 protein promotes the binding of oncogenic transcription factors, such as c-Myc, to the *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* gene promoters, leading to their activation and subsequent degradation of the miRNA transcripts.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male smoker with symptoms suggestive of lung cancer (cough, shortness of breath). Smoking is a major risk factor for lung cancer, especially squamous cell carcinoma.
- **Diagnosis:** Squamous cell carcinoma of the lung confirmed by biopsy.
- **Molecular Finding:** Missense mutation (R248Q) in the *TP53* gene, resulting in a non-functional p53 protein. This is a common finding in many cancers.
- **miRNA Analysis:** Significantly reduced expression of miR-34a and miR-34b in the tumor cells compared to normal tissue.
- **Protein Confirmation:** Western blot confirms decreased levels of the miR-34a protein (part of the RISC complex) in tumor cells.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the reduced miR-34a/b expression due to non-functional p53.

The key link is the known role of p53 as a transcription factor that directly activates the transcription of the *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* genes, which encode tumor suppressor miRNAs. The patient has a non-functional p53 protein due to a *TP53* mutation. Therefore, the lack of functional p53 protein will prevent the transcription of these miRNA genes.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **p53 Function:** p53 is a crucial tumor suppressor protein, often called the "guardian of the genome." It acts as a transcription factor, activated in response to cellular stress (e.g., DNA damage, oncogene activation).
- **p53 Transcriptional Regulation:** Upon activation (e.g., phosphorylation), p53 forms tetramers and binds to specific DNA sequences (p53 response elements) in the promoter/enhancer regions of target genes.
- **miR-34 Family Regulation by p53:** The *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* genes (located on chromosomes 1p13.3 and 1q32.1, respectively) are direct transcriptional targets of p53. Activated p53 binds to E2F response elements and p53 response elements within the promoter/enhancer regions of these genes, leading to increased transcription of primary miR-34 transcripts (pri-miRNAs).
- **miRNA Biogenesis:** Pri-miRNAs are processed in the nucleus by Drosha and DGCR8 to form precursor miRNAs (pre-miRNAs), which are then exported to the cytoplasm. In the cytoplasm, Dicer processes pre-miRNAs into mature miRNA duplexes. One strand is selected to become the mature miRNA, which is incorporated into the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC).
- **miR-34 Function:** Mature miR-34a and miR-34b are tumor suppressor miRNAs. They regulate the expression of numerous target genes involved in cell cycle progression, apoptosis, DNA repair, and metastasis (e.g., *CDK4*, *CDK6*, *BIRC5*, *MET*, *SIRT1*).
- **Consequence of p53 Inactivation:** Inactivation of p53 (e.g., by mutation, deletion, or inactivation of its regulators like MDM2) leads to decreased transcription of *MIR34A* and *MIR34B*. This results in reduced levels of mature miR-34a/b, leading to upregulation of their target genes and promoting tumor development and progression.
- **Applying to the Vignette:** The patient has a non-functional p53 protein due to a *TP53* mutation. This means p53 cannot bind to the regulatory regions of the *MIR34A* and *MIR34B* genes. Consequently, the transcription of these genes is reduced, leading to decreased levels of miR-34a and miR-34b, as observed in the patient's tumor.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While promoter methylation can silence genes, including tumor suppressors, the primary mechanism by which p53 regulates *MIR34A/B* is direct transcriptional activation via binding to their promoter/enhancer regions. Loss of p53 function *prevents* this activation, not methylation. Methylation is not the *direct* consequence of p53 loss in this context.
- (C): **Incorrect.** A dominant-negative p53 protein would interfere with the function of any remaining wild-type p53 protein (if present in a heterozygous state). However, the primary mechanism of miR-34 downregulation is the *loss* of p53's transcriptional activation function, not sequestration of other transcription factors. Furthermore, the vignette states the mutation leads to a *non-functional* protein, which doesn't necessarily imply dominant-negative activity, although some mutations can be. The core mechanism is the loss of transcriptional activation.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Misfolding and degradation of p53 can occur with certain mutations, but the *direct* consequence of losing functional p53 is the inability to activate transcription. While changes in epigenetic modifiers like HDACs can occur in cancer, they are not the primary, direct mechanism linking non-functional p53 to reduced *MIR34A/B* transcription. p53 itself can influence HDAC activity, but the loss of p53's direct binding to the *MIR34A/B* promoters is the key event here.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes a scenario where p53 is functional but promotes the *activation* of *MIR34A/B*, leading to miRNA degradation. This is the opposite of what happens when p53 is non-functional. Non-functional p53 *fails* to activate *MIR34A/B* transcription, leading to reduced miRNA levels. Oncogenic transcription factors like c-Myc can sometimes repress miRNAs, but this is not the mechanism described here, and the primary defect is the loss of p53's activating role.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Remember that p53 is a transcription factor that directly activates the transcription of tumor suppressor miRNAs like the miR-34 family. Loss of p53 function leads to downregulation of these miRNAs, contributing to cancer development. (记住 p53 是一个转录因子，直接激活肿瘤抑制剂 microRNA (如 miR-34 家族) 的转录。p53 功能丧失导致这些 microRNA 下调，从而促进癌症发展。)
- **Differentials:** Other mechanisms can regulate miRNA expression (e.g., promoter methylation, processing defects, target gene upregulation), but the specific link between p53 and miR-34 involves direct transcriptional activation. Understand the p53 pathway and its downstream targets, including miRNAs. (其他机制可以调节 microRNA 表达 (例如，启动子甲基化、加工缺陷、靶基因上调)，但 p53 和 miR-34 之间的特定联系涉及直接的转录激活。理解 p53 通路及其下游靶点，包括 microRNA。)

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Integrated Clinical Case: Fulminant Hepatic Failure Following Wild Mushroom Ingestion Inhibiting Transcription - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Inhibition of RNA polymerase II by amatoxins (found in *Amanita* mushrooms) leads to decreased protein synthesis, particularly affecting hepatocytes, causing acute liver failure. (鹅膏毒素抑制RNA聚合酶II导致蛋白质合成减少，特别是肝细胞，引起急性肝衰竭)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the emergency department 12 hours after consuming wild mushrooms foraged in a local park. He initially felt well but developed severe nausea, vomiting, abdominal cramps, and watery diarrhea approximately 6 hours post-ingestion. Over the next 6 hours, he became increasingly lethargic, jaundiced, and confused. His vital signs show a heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and temperature of 37.5°C. Physical examination reveals scleral icterus, ascites, and signs of hepatic encephalopathy (asterixis, altered mental status). Laboratory tests show markedly elevated serum aminotransferases (AST 1500 U/L, ALT 1800 U/L), elevated bilirubin (total 12 mg/dL, direct 8 mg/dL), prolonged prothrombin time (PT 60 seconds, INR 5.0), low serum albumin (2.0 g/dL), and elevated ammonia levels (150 µmol/L). A liver biopsy reveals centrilobular necrosis. The patient rapidly progresses to multi-organ failure and death within 48 hours despite supportive care. Which of the following is the most likely mechanism underlying this patient's fulminant hepatic failure?

(A) Inhibition of mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation, leading to microvesicular steatosis and hepatocyte dysfunction.
(B) Inhibition of RNA polymerase I, leading to decreased ribosomal RNA synthesis and impaired protein translation.
(C) Inhibition of RNA polymerase II, leading to decreased mRNA synthesis and impaired protein translation, particularly affecting rapidly dividing cells like hepatocytes.
(D) Inhibition of DNA polymerase, leading to impaired DNA replication and cell death, primarily affecting rapidly dividing cells.
(E) Inhibition of protein synthesis by binding to the 60S ribosomal subunit, causing a buildup of misfolded proteins in the endoplasmic reticulum.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male, ingestion of wild mushrooms, delayed onset (6-12 hours) of severe GI symptoms (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, cramps), followed by rapid progression to jaundice, confusion, lethargy, ascites, hepatic encephalopathy (asterixis), and multi-organ failure within 48 hours. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of acute fulminant hepatic failure.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated AST/ALT (indicating hepatocellular injury), elevated bilirubin (indicating impaired bilirubin metabolism/excretion), prolonged PT/INR (indicating impaired synthesis of clotting factors), low albumin (indicating impaired protein synthesis), elevated ammonia (indicating impaired urea cycle function and detoxification), and centrilobular necrosis on biopsy (characteristic pattern of liver damage).
- **Connecting Clues:** The ingestion of wild mushrooms, the specific timing and progression of symptoms (GI distress followed by rapid liver failure), and the pattern of liver injury (centrilobular necrosis) strongly point towards amatoxin poisoning, commonly associated with *Amanita* species. Amatoxins are known inhibitors of RNA polymerase II.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *mechanism* underlying the fulminant hepatic failure.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Amatoxins:** These are cyclic peptides found in certain poisonous mushrooms, notably *Amanita phalloides* (death cap) and *Amanita virosa* (destroying angel).
- **Mechanism of Action:** Amatoxins, specifically α-amanitin, are potent inhibitors of RNA polymerase II (Pol II). Pol II is responsible for transcribing messenger RNA (mRNA) from DNA templates.
- **Cellular Impact:** Inhibition of Pol II halts mRNA synthesis. This affects the production of virtually all proteins, but it particularly impacts cells with high rates of protein synthesis and turnover, such as hepatocytes (liver cells) and enterocytes (intestinal cells).
- **Pathophysiology:**
    1.  **Initial GI Phase (6-24 hours):** Amatoxins inhibit protein synthesis in enterocytes, leading to cell damage and death, causing severe gastroenteritis (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea).
    2.  **Latent Phase (24-48 hours):** The toxin is absorbed and distributed to the liver.
    3.  **Hepatic Phase (48-72 hours):** Amatoxins accumulate in hepatocytes and inhibit RNA Pol II, drastically reducing the synthesis of essential hepatocyte proteins (e.g., albumin, clotting factors, cytochrome P450 enzymes). This leads to impaired liver function, hepatocellular necrosis (often centrilobular), jaundice, coagulopathy, encephalopathy, and ultimately, fulminant hepatic failure.
- **Why RNA Pol II?** RNA Pol II transcribes protein-coding genes, ribosomal RNA genes (rRNA), and some small RNA genes. Inhibition of Pol II directly blocks the production of mRNA needed for protein synthesis. Hepatocytes are highly metabolically active and rely heavily on continuous protein synthesis, making them exquisitely sensitive to Pol II inhibition.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Inhibition of mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation leads to microvesicular steatosis (fatty liver) and can cause hypoglycemia and liver dysfunction, but it doesn't typically cause the rapid, severe centrilobular necrosis and fulminant failure seen here. This mechanism is more characteristic of conditions like Reye's syndrome or certain drug toxicities (e.g., valproic acid). The clinical picture and specific toxin point away from this.
- (B): Inhibition of RNA polymerase I (Pol I) would primarily affect the synthesis of ribosomal RNA (rRNA), which is crucial for ribosome biogenesis and protein translation. While this would impair protein synthesis, Pol I is less sensitive to amatoxins than Pol II. Furthermore, the primary target of amatoxins is Pol II, and the clinical presentation aligns perfectly with Pol II inhibition affecting protein-synthesizing cells like hepatocytes.
- (D): Inhibition of DNA polymerase would impair DNA replication, primarily affecting rapidly dividing cells (e.g., bone marrow, intestinal crypt cells, cancer cells). While amatoxins can affect rapidly dividing cells, their primary and most potent effect is on RNA Pol II, not DNA polymerase. The liver damage pattern and the specific toxin involved make this incorrect.
- (E): Inhibition of protein synthesis by binding to the 60S ribosomal subunit is the mechanism of action for certain antibiotics (e.g., chloramphenicol) and toxins (e.g., ricin). While this would impair protein synthesis, it's not the mechanism of amatoxins. Amatoxins act *before* translation, at the level of transcription by inhibiting RNA Pol II.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Amatoxin poisoning (from *Amanita* mushrooms) causes fulminant hepatic failure by inhibiting RNA polymerase II, leading to decreased mRNA synthesis and subsequent protein synthesis failure, particularly in hepatocytes.
- **Differentials:** Other causes of acute liver failure include viral hepatitis (HAV, HBV, HCV, HEV), drug-induced liver injury (e.g., acetaminophen overdose), autoimmune hepatitis, Wilson's disease, and ischemic hepatitis. However, the specific history of wild mushroom ingestion points strongly towards amatoxin poisoning. Other mushroom toxins exist (e.g., gyromitrin inhibiting PDH, orellanine causing renal failure), but amatoxins are the classic cause of this specific clinical syndrome.

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: tRNA Processing Defects
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: tRNA processing involves cleavage of the 5' leader sequence by RNase P and the 3' trailer sequence by RNase Z. Defects in these enzymes lead to accumulation of precursor tRNAs and impaired protein synthesis. (tRNA加工包括RNase P切割5'端leader序列和RNase Z切割3'端trailer序列。这些酶的缺陷会导致前体tRNA积累和蛋白质合成受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric clinic with developmental delay, hypotonia, microcephaly, and failure to thrive. His parents report that he has been feeding poorly since infancy and has recurrent respiratory infections. Family history is unremarkable. Physical examination reveals a height and weight below the 3rd percentile, decreased muscle tone, and a head circumference below the 5th percentile. Laboratory investigations show elevated plasma lactate and pyruvate levels. A skin fibroblast biopsy is performed for further analysis. RNA analysis from the fibroblasts reveals a significant accumulation of precursor tRNA molecules with intact 5' leader sequences and 3' trailer sequences, while mature tRNA levels are markedly reduced. Sequencing of the gene encoding the enzyme responsible for cleaving the 3' end of precursor tRNAs reveals a homozygous missense mutation leading to a non-functional protein.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed biochemical and clinical findings in this patient?

(A) Impaired aminoacylation of tRNA due to a defect in aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase.
(B) Defective tRNA charging leading to reduced protein synthesis due to a defect in the mitochondrial ATP synthase complex.
(C) Accumulation of precursor tRNAs with intact 5' leader and 3' trailer sequences due to a defect in the enzyme responsible for 3' end processing.
(D) Impaired tRNA folding and maturation due to a defect in the enzyme responsible for modifying the anticodon loop.
(E) Reduced availability of mature tRNAs due to a defect in the enzyme responsible for cleaving the 5' leader sequence.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, microcephaly, failure to thrive, poor feeding, recurrent respiratory infections. These symptoms suggest a systemic metabolic disorder affecting growth and development, potentially involving energy metabolism or protein synthesis.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated plasma lactate and pyruvate suggest impaired mitochondrial function or energy metabolism.
- **Fibroblast RNA Analysis:** Accumulation of precursor tRNAs with intact 5' leader and 3' trailer sequences, and reduced mature tRNA levels. This is the crucial clue pointing directly to a defect in tRNA processing. The presence of both leader and trailer sequences indicates that *both* 5' and 3' cleavage steps might be affected, or that the specific enzyme defect prevents the final maturation step after one cleavage. However, the question stem focuses on the *specific* enzyme defect identified by sequencing.
- **Genetic Finding:** Homozygous missense mutation in the gene encoding the enzyme responsible for cleaving the 3' end of precursor tRNAs, leading to a non-functional protein. This identifies the specific molecular defect: a loss-of-function mutation in RNase Z.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The clinical symptoms (developmental delay, hypotonia, failure to thrive) are consistent with impaired protein synthesis, which can result from defective tRNA maturation. The lab findings (elevated lactate/pyruvate) can be secondary to impaired cellular energy production due to reduced protein synthesis or mitochondrial dysfunction. The RNA analysis confirms a defect in tRNA processing, specifically the accumulation of precursors with both leader and trailer sequences. The genetic finding pinpoints the defect to the enzyme responsible for 3' end cleavage (RNase Z). Therefore, the accumulation of precursors with both leader and trailer sequences is due to the non-functional RNase Z, which normally cleaves the 3' trailer. The 5' leader is cleaved by RNase P, which is presumably functional here, but the final mature tRNA cannot be formed without the 3' cleavage step.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **tRNA Processing:** Transfer RNAs (tRNAs) are transcribed as longer precursor molecules (pre-tRNAs) that require extensive processing to become mature, functional tRNAs.
- **5' End Processing:** The 5' leader sequence is cleaved off by the RNase P complex, a ribonucleoprotein enzyme acting as a ribozyme (the RNA component has catalytic activity). This cleavage generates the mature 5' end of the tRNA.
- **3' End Processing:** The 3' trailer sequence is cleaved off by the enzyme RNase Z (also known as PACE). This cleavage generates the mature 3' CCA sequence, which is essential for aminoacylation.
- **Other Modifications:** Mature tRNAs also undergo numerous base modifications (e.g., methylation, pseudouridylation) and folding, which are crucial for their structure and function.
- **Molecular Mechanism in the Patient:** The patient has a homozygous loss-of-function mutation in the gene encoding RNase Z. This enzyme is responsible for cleaving the 3' trailer sequence from pre-tRNAs. Without functional RNase Z, the 3' trailer sequence remains attached to the pre-tRNA molecule, even after the 5' leader sequence has been cleaved by RNase P. This results in the accumulation of pre-tRNAs with both the 5' leader and 3' trailer sequences, as observed in the fibroblast RNA analysis. The lack of mature tRNAs with the correct 3' CCA end impairs aminoacylation and subsequent protein synthesis, leading to the observed clinical symptoms (developmental delay, hypotonia, failure to thrive). The elevated lactate and pyruvate may reflect impaired cellular energy production secondary to reduced protein synthesis or potentially a direct effect on mitochondrial function, although the primary defect is in tRNA processing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired aminoacylation of tRNA due to a defect in aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase. Aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases are responsible for attaching the correct amino acid to the 3' end of the tRNA. While a defect here would impair protein synthesis, it wouldn't lead to the accumulation of pre-tRNAs with intact leader and trailer sequences. The defect described in the vignette is specifically in the processing of the pre-tRNA molecule itself, not in the charging step.
- (B) Defective tRNA charging leading to reduced protein synthesis due to a defect in the mitochondrial ATP synthase complex. tRNA charging is the process of aminoacylation. A defect in mitochondrial ATP synthase would primarily affect ATP production, leading to energy deficiency, but it doesn't directly explain the accumulation of specific pre-tRNA species with intact leader and trailer sequences. While impaired energy production can affect protein synthesis, the specific finding of unprocessed pre-tRNAs points to a defect in tRNA processing enzymes.
- (D) Impaired tRNA folding and maturation due to a defect in the enzyme responsible for modifying the anticodon loop. tRNA folding and modifications (like those in the anticodon loop) are essential for tRNA structure and function. Defects in these processes would impair tRNA function but typically wouldn't lead to the accumulation of pre-tRNAs with both leader and trailer sequences. The vignette specifically describes a defect in cleavage, not modification or folding.
- (E) Reduced availability of mature tRNAs due to a defect in the enzyme responsible for cleaving the 5' leader sequence. The enzyme responsible for cleaving the 5' leader sequence is RNase P. A defect in RNase P would lead to the accumulation of pre-tRNAs with intact 5' leader sequences, but the 3' trailer sequence would still be present (unless RNase Z was also affected, which is not the case here). The vignette explicitly states the defect is in the enzyme cleaving the 3' end and identifies the accumulation of precursors with *both* leader and trailer sequences, ruling out an isolated RNase P defect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** tRNA processing involves sequential cleavage of the 5' leader (by RNase P) and 3' trailer (by RNase Z) from pre-tRNA transcripts. Defects in these enzymes lead to accumulation of precursor tRNAs and impaired protein synthesis, manifesting clinically as developmental delay, hypotonia, and failure to thrive.
- **Differentials:** Consider other disorders of tRNA metabolism, such as defects in aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases (leading to specific amino acid deficiencies) or defects in tRNA modifications (leading to impaired codon recognition or translation fidelity). However, the specific finding of accumulated pre-tRNAs with both leader and trailer sequences points directly to a defect in the 3' end cleavage enzyme, RNase Z.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: tRNA Nucleotidyltransferase: Template-Independent Addition of CCA-OH Triplet to 3' End of All tRNAs - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: tRNA processing, specifically the template-independent addition of the CCA sequence to the 3' end by tRNA nucleotidyltransferase (CCA-adding enzyme), and the consequences of its deficiency. tRNA 剪接 (tRNA processing), 特别是 CCA 序列加到 3' 端 (template-independent addition of CCA sequence to the 3' end) 以及 tRNA 核苷酸转移酶 (CCA-adding enzyme) 缺乏的后果 (consequences of its deficiency).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents to the pediatric emergency department with failure to thrive, developmental delay, microcephaly, and recurrent infections. The infant was born full-term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery to healthy, unrelated parents. Family history is unremarkable. Physical examination reveals hypotonia, dysmorphic facial features (including a prominent forehead and hypertelorism), and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory investigations show severe anemia (hemoglobin 6 g/dL), leukopenia (WBC count 2,000/µL), and thrombocytopenia (platelet count 50,000/µL). Bone marrow aspiration and biopsy reveal pancytopenia with dysplastic changes in all three cell lines, including megaloblastic features in erythroid precursors. A peripheral blood smear shows macrocytic red blood cells and hypersegmented neutrophils. Analysis of tRNA levels reveals significantly reduced levels of all mature tRNAs in peripheral blood lymphocytes. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous missense mutation in the *CCAAT* gene, which encodes the tRNA nucleotidyltransferase enzyme.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pancytopenia and dysplastic hematopoiesis in this infant?

(A) Impaired amino acid charging of initiator tRNA, leading to defective translation initiation and global protein synthesis shutdown.
(B) Reduced levels of mature tRNA molecules, resulting in impaired protein synthesis and subsequent cellular dysfunction, particularly affecting rapidly dividing cells like hematopoietic stem cells.
(C) Accumulation of pre-tRNA molecules lacking the CCA tail, which competitively inhibit the function of other essential enzymes involved in RNA processing.
(D) Defective splicing of mRNA transcripts due to the lack of CCA-tailed tRNAs involved in mRNA maturation.
(E) Increased degradation of mature tRNA molecules by aberrant ribonucleases due to the absence of the CCA tail, which normally protects tRNAs from degradation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-month-old infant with failure to thrive, developmental delay, microcephaly, recurrent infections, hypotonia, dysmorphic features, hepatosplenomegaly. These symptoms suggest a severe congenital disorder affecting multiple organ systems, potentially involving growth, development, and immunity.
- **Hematologic Findings:** Severe pancytopenia (anemia, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia) with dysplastic changes (megaloblastic features, hypersegmented neutrophils) in the bone marrow. This points towards a defect in hematopoiesis, specifically affecting rapidly dividing cells. Megaloblastic features often suggest impaired DNA synthesis or nucleotide metabolism.
- **Biochemical Findings:** Significantly reduced levels of all mature tRNAs in peripheral blood lymphocytes. This is a crucial clue indicating a global defect in tRNA maturation or processing.
- **Genetic Findings:** Homozygous missense mutation in the *CCAAT* gene, encoding tRNA nucleotidyltransferase (CCA-adding enzyme). This identifies the specific enzyme deficiency responsible for the observed phenotype.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The *CCAAT* gene encodes the enzyme responsible for adding the CCA sequence to the 3' end of all tRNA molecules. A deficiency in this enzyme leads to a lack of the CCA tail on tRNAs. The CCA tail is essential for amino acid attachment (charging) by aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases. Reduced levels of mature tRNAs, lacking the CCA tail, impair protein synthesis. This impairment is particularly severe in rapidly dividing cells, such as hematopoietic stem cells, leading to pancytopenia and dysplastic hematopoiesis (megaloblastic changes). The global nature of the tRNA defect explains the widespread symptoms (failure to thrive, developmental delay, microcephaly, recurrent infections).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **tRNA Nucleotidyltransferase Function:** This enzyme (encoded by *CCAAT* in eukaryotes) catalyzes the template-independent addition of the sequence CCA-3'OH to the 3' end of all tRNA molecules. This process occurs after tRNA transcription and initial processing.
- **CCA Tail Importance:** The CCA tail is absolutely essential for the function of tRNAs. It provides the 3'-hydroxyl group required for the attachment of the correct amino acid by the specific aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase. Without the CCA tail, the tRNA cannot be "charged" with its corresponding amino acid.
- **Consequences of Deficiency:** A deficiency in tRNA nucleotidyltransferase leads to the accumulation of pre-tRNAs lacking the CCA tail. These pre-tRNAs cannot be charged with amino acids and are therefore non-functional. The lack of functional, charged tRNAs severely impairs protein synthesis in all cells, but particularly affects rapidly dividing cells like those in the bone marrow, intestinal crypts, and developing nervous system.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The impaired protein synthesis explains the failure to thrive, developmental delay, microcephaly, and susceptibility to infections. The severe effect on hematopoietic stem cells leads to pancytopenia with megaloblastic features, mimicking vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, although the underlying mechanism is different (impaired protein synthesis, not impaired DNA synthesis directly).
- **Mechanism Linking to Pancytopenia:** Hematopoietic stem cells are highly proliferative. Protein synthesis is crucial for cell division, differentiation, and survival. The lack of functional tRNAs due to the CCA-adding enzyme deficiency severely compromises protein synthesis in these cells, leading to ineffective hematopoiesis, apoptosis, and ultimately pancytopenia with dysplastic features.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired amino acid charging of *initiator* tRNA (tRNAiMet) would primarily affect translation initiation. While this would impair protein synthesis, the vignette describes a *global* reduction in *all* mature tRNAs, not just the initiator tRNA. Furthermore, the primary defect is the lack of the CCA tail, preventing charging of *all* tRNAs, not just the initiator. The clinical picture (pancytopenia, developmental delay) is consistent with global protein synthesis impairment, but the specific mechanism described in (A) is incorrect for a CCA-adding enzyme deficiency.
- (C): Accumulation of pre-tRNA molecules lacking the CCA tail *does* occur. However, the primary consequence is not competitive inhibition of other enzymes. The main problem is the *lack* of functional, charged tRNAs available for protein synthesis. While pre-tRNAs might have other roles or fates, competitive inhibition is not the established mechanism causing the severe clinical phenotype. The lack of charged tRNAs is the direct cause of impaired protein synthesis.
- (D): Defective splicing of mRNA transcripts is typically caused by mutations in splicing factors or splice sites, not by a lack of CCA-tailed tRNAs. While tRNAs are involved in translation, they are not directly involved in the splicing process of mRNA. This option confuses the roles of tRNA and splicing machinery.
- (E): While the CCA tail is essential for aminoacylation, the primary issue is the *lack* of the tail itself, preventing charging. The vignette states reduced levels of *mature* tRNAs. While aberrant degradation might occur secondarily, the fundamental problem is the failure to produce mature, functional tRNAs with the CCA tail in the first place. The lack of the CCA tail prevents charging, which is the critical step for tRNA function in translation. Therefore, the lack of charging, not increased degradation, is the direct consequence of the enzyme deficiency leading to the phenotype.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** A deficiency in tRNA nucleotidyltransferase (CCA-adding enzyme) prevents the addition of the CCA tail to all tRNAs, rendering them non-functional because they cannot be aminoacylated. This leads to impaired protein synthesis, particularly affecting rapidly dividing cells, resulting in severe developmental defects and pancytopenia.
- **Differentials:** This condition can mimic severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to recurrent infections and developmental delay, or megaloblastic anemia (B12/folate deficiency) due to pancytopenia with megaloblastic features. However, the underlying biochemical defect (tRNA processing) is distinct. Other tRNA-related disorders (e.g., mutations in aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases) can also cause disease, but the specific enzyme defect here is the CCA-adding enzyme.
